October 22 test-build retest

Updated Oct 22, 2026, 3:48 PM Eastern. Windows 0.1.100 and Android 1.51-tester35 (build 85) are installed test builds, not public downloads. At that time the public downloads were Windows 0.1.99 and Android tester34; Windows 0.1.102 was published later (Oct 24, 2026, 1:05 PM Eastern) and tester35 was not. The coordinator reports viewer/server repairs deployed October 22 at 6:49 AM Eastern and the older-TV-browser navigation repair at 7:24 AM Eastern. RETEST-1 reports 14 of 18 items passed; the other four include a child delete-screen failure, sidecar subtitles selected but not drawn, inconsistent online Next and network-dependent offline wording. Folder poster/NFO handling has no recorded repair. No new feature tick is justified.

The browser repair has split delivery: the server portion is reported deployed at 6:49 AM Eastern on October 22, with a further TV-browser server repair at 7:24 AM; the desktop-player portion is in unpublished Windows 0.1.100. The affected PC and combined away-route repair still need retesting.

Owner-reported on October 22: on a friend’s Samsung TV built-in browser, playback away from home and Skip intro worked after repeated Reconnect clicks; ordinary poster/sidebar navigation failed without that extra step, and Up next/Next were absent at the credits. The fixed-build retry, exact TV model, browser version and served build are unconfirmed. Earlier Chromecast viewing is a separate observation with sender details unconfirmed. No new tick or broad real-world pass.

Feature-by-feature evidence and remaining checks. Historical checkmarks retain their original build and scope; no new tick or Available upgrade.

Site last updated: Oct 24, 2026, 9:15 PM Eastern · 13 features changed since the previous update

Testing transparency

Evidence, not assumptions.

Beebo uses local checks, AI-assisted hand testing and human reports to find problems. They are useful together—but no one kind of testing proves every device, route or feature.

Every published result needs a build, a scope and an honest limit. A human tester is still needed on every release.

Newer: Oct 24 afternoon and evening real-world checks: a Galaxy S24 on 5G reached the owner’s home server directly and through Beebo Relay and a real second household’s PC through Beebo Relay; the cast controls and a streamed film worked on the real TV in unpublished test builds; and the browser player drew separate subtitles. Failures were recorded alongside: a converted 720p quality failed twice on the second household’s old PC, casting a downloaded copy did not play, Watch Together did not connect for phones signed in only to the home server, Trip Journal video export stayed at 0%, and the web player had keyboard, show-page, blank Suggestions and owner-only-control problems. No percentage, checkmark or Ready changed. Final phone round five records 127 PASS, 15 FAIL and 16 BLOCKED assessments across 32 rows. It carries 110 earlier passes without rerunning them. The separate final PC campaign records 28 PASS, one FAIL and four BLOCKED scopes; its 27 earlier passes and the older 97-assessment Windows/web record remain history. Cross-device totals overlap and must not be added. The published Memory Match wording fix passed real hearts/spades and game-completion acceptance, resolving its sole issue cap and restoring the games checklist to 99%. No checkmark or Ready was added. Seven installed-phone bugs remain open, including native Cast media loading; the silent Live TV failure is the same unresolved Cast issue. Native tester36 checks cover26 of32 rows, including failures; they do not accept newer source. The installed Windows application was never launched. Actual retention expiry, low-space/first-visit work, installed/client fixtures, hearing and hardware gaps remain explicit.

Where Beebo stands · updated Oct 22, 2026, 6:47 AM Eastern

The code is about 92% done; most of what is left is real-world testing.

The code for nearly everything that exists today is written and passes its automated tests. What is left is trying it on more real phones, TVs and PCs, and in real situations such as a real car or a friend’s Wi-Fi. A few items are still being built or reviewed: 34 code items are still listed, 9 of them reviews, and each one is written under its feature below. The figures count all 70 features, including those that are planned or on hold.

Code built and passing automated tests92%
Tested on real devices26%

70 features: 0 confirmed working on real devices, 35 code complete and waiting for real-world testing, 35 code still being finished.

What the badges and numbers mean

  • ✔Confirmed working on real devices Used only when the Real device check, the Real-world check and the Ready to call Available check have all passed, with no code item open. It means all listed checks passed. It does not mean bug-free.
  • ◐Code complete — needs more real-world testing to confirm The code is built, its automated tests pass and no code item is open, but at least one of the real-device, real-world or ready checks has not passed yet.
  • ⚙︎Code still being finished At least one code item is still open (building, fixing or an independent review), or the Built or Automated tests check has not passed.

The first number, “Code”: the share of the two code checks (Built and Automated tests) that passed. A feature reads “Code 100%” only when both passed and no code item is still open for it. The second number, “Tested on real devices”: the share of the three real-world checks that passed. The percentages measure our own checklist, not that nothing can go wrong. We do not call anything 100% working.

✔Confirmed working on real devices 0

No feature has passed all five checks yet, so none is confirmed.

Real-world checks recorded Oct 24, 2026, 9:15 PM Eastern

October 24: away from home, a second household, the TV and the browser player. What passed, what failed.

These are real-device checks run by AI helpers on Oct 24, 2026, afternoon and evening, with the households’ owners approving the sign-ins and the relay terms. Most phone checks used an unpublished phone test build (tester40 to tester42), not a published download; the Windows PCs ran the published Windows 0.1.102. Each line says what was tried and what was not. A test that was not run is unknown, not broken. No percentage, checkmark or status label changed.

Real-world checks of Oct 24, 2026, in time order
Device and buildWhen (Eastern)ResultWhat passed, what failed, what was not tested
Galaxy S24 and the Living Room TV; unpublished phone test buildabout 1:00 to 2:40 PMPassed (cast controls, after a fix); one failure found firstWith the owner watching the Living Room TV: the on-screen play, pause and skip controls were dimmed and could not be tapped while casting (a TV-volume row added on Oct 20 had grown the top bar over the buttons). The fix was reviewed and merged into the main code line at about 3:45 PM; it is in test builds only. On the real device the owner paused and played by finger, skipped, opened the quality menu and stopped casting. Not tested: seeking by dragging, captions on the TV, a second sender.
Galaxy S24 on 5G mobile data only (Wi-Fi off), owner’s home server; unpublished phone test buildabout 1:30 PMPassed (direct, no relay)Played a TV episode directly with no relay: the clock advanced 21 seconds in 20 seconds with no stall, and the route screen read “Direct over the internet, no relay”. Not tested: Wi-Fi to mobile-data hand-over, sessions over a few minutes.
Same phone, “always use Beebo Relay” switched onabout 3:40 PMPlayed, slower than real timeThe route screen read “Beebo Relay” and a film played over 5G, but the clock advanced only 12 seconds in 20 seconds and nothing longer was measured, so this is not a smooth-playback result.
Galaxy S24 on 5G to a real second household’s old 4-core Windows PC (Windows 0.1.102, published; the PC had updated itself to it at 5:41 PM)about 7:50 to 8:05 PMPassed (original file through Beebo Relay); failed (converted 720p, twice)The household owner accepted the relay terms with the new “Use Beebo Relay” button; that PC’s log moved from “relay beebo unavailable” to “relay beebo ready”. The S24 signed in through that household’s Beebo address, browsed its library, connected through Beebo Relay in about 2 seconds and played the original file of a film with picture and sound (clock 0:51 to 1:32 in 40 seconds). Choosing a converted 720p quality failed twice (“Playback interrupted”; the conversion session opened and stopped, and the log did not say why). Not tested: Wi-Fi to mobile-data hand-over, sessions over a few minutes, a converted quality that works on that PC, a direct route to that PC. Whether the phone’s always-use-Relay switch was on for this run is not recorded. Casting on mobile data shows an honest “Casting needs Wi-Fi” message.
Galaxy S24 and the Living Room TV; unpublished test build 42about 8:20 PMPassed (streamed film); failed (downloaded copy)A streamed film cast and played on the TV with picture and sound; pause, play, a 15-second skip forward and stop casting worked, and the TV left cast mode. Casting a downloaded copy showed “Casting” on the phone but the TV did not play (a TV cannot reach a file saved on the phone); an honest message is a small open item. Still unproven: the phone pass-through route (it needs the phone on a different network from the home computer; a fix for a likely cause is written and passes 16 new tests but is not merged), seeking by dragging, captions on the TV, a second sender.
Browser player on a Galaxy S24 over 5G through Beebo Relay (home-server Windows 0.1.102)about 8:25 PMPassedA tiny synthetic test film with a separate subtitle file: the subtitle menu listed “English (file)”, and three cues drew at the right times (4.3, 9.7 and 15.0 seconds) and again after seeking back. This is the browser player. Not tested: the Android app’s own captions (a fix was merged into the code line that evening and is in test build 42, not yet seen on a phone), burned-in captions on converted video, other subtitle formats.
Chrome on a PC, against the home serverOct 24 (time not recorded)Passed in part; 4 failures (fixes written, not merged)Passed with no Beebo errors in the browser console: playback, pause, Left and Right arrow (minus and plus 10 seconds), quality change 480p, 720p, 480p, search, By Actor, By Release Date, Music, Audiobooks, Playlists and Watchlist. Failed: Space does not pause and F does not go full screen unless the video has focus; a show page has no poster and its Playlist, Play next and Add to queue buttons are dark text on dark (contrast 1.4 to 1; 4.5 is needed); the Suggestions page loads blank; Media requests shows a household member owner-only controls (the server side was not yet proven to refuse them). Two dark posters looked blank but load fine. An automated browser test now exists (10 checks); its first run passed 4 and failed 6, matching the hand findings.
Two phones signed in only to the home server (household members), Watch TogetherOct 24 (time not recorded)FailedHost and Join looked connected but never joined, because the party service needs a cloud sign-in. Test build 41 now says “Watch Together could not connect. Sign in with your Beebo account to use it.” and stops retrying: an honest error, not a working room. The owner decided it should work for anyone with access; a design is written and nothing is built. No two-person sync pass.
Galaxy S9+, Trip Journal “Save as a video”Oct 24 (time not recorded)FailedWith 2 cards, 2 photos and 1 short clip, progress stayed at 0%, and the app retried a smaller size and gave up after about 30 seconds each time. The cause is not yet known and is being investigated. No pass is claimed.
Campsite: Galaxy S9+ host on home Wi-Fi, Galaxy S24 guest in Chrome (no app, no account)Oct 24 (time not recorded)Passed in scopeThe guest typed a name and joined; the host showed “1 guest connected”; the guest page showed the host’s library (644 titles) with Movies, Music and Games. On mobile data only, the same page gave an honest “can’t reach the host” message. Not tested: a guest game round, guest video playback, a hotspot with a SIM guest.

Other items from Oct 24. Windows 0.1.102 was published at 1:05 PM Eastern. A second, older 4-core Windows PC updated itself from 0.1.99 to 0.1.102 at 5:41 PM (the download matched the feed, the installer signature checked as valid, it installed in about a minute), and its log shows Start with Windows launching the app three times between Oct 22 and Oct 24. The Android photo map was decided as a build (opt-in, your own photos only, map tiles fetched through the home computer only); it is built in a branch with 27 automated tests and a test build exists, but it is not merged, not released and not yet seen on a phone with geotagged photos. A review of the new library-share code route (eight characters, 100-person cap) found two high-severity limiter and lock problems and several smaller ones; a repair branch is pushed but not cleared, merged or deployed, and the route is off.

What this changed in the numbers. Nothing: no check was added or withdrawn, no status label changed, and the average across all 70 features stays at 70%. The 13 rows with new dated evidence below keep their percentages. The earlier wording that the Chromecast “connected without a receiver media session” describes the older routes tried on Oct 23; on Oct 24 a streamed film cast with picture and sound on test build 42, in an unpublished build.

Not tested in these runs: Wi-Fi to mobile-data hand-over, sessions longer than a few minutes, two people on Beebo Relay at once, a converted quality that works on the second PC, a direct route to the second PC, the phone pass-through cast route, seeking by dragging or captions on the TV, the Android app’s own captions, burned-in captions, a guest game round or guest video in Campsite, a hotspot with a SIM guest, the Android photo map on a phone with geotagged photos, and any sound heard by a person on a phone.

On the PC: a browser that could not open a film through the away address, and a folder’s own posters and NFO files that were not used. Windows 0.1.100 and Android 1.51-tester35 (build 85) are installed test builds, not public downloads. At that time the public downloads were Windows 0.1.99 and Android tester34; Windows 0.1.102 was published later (Oct 24, 2026, 1:05 PM Eastern) and tester35 was not. The coordinator reports viewer/server repairs deployed October 22 at 6:49 AM Eastern and the older-TV-browser navigation repair at 7:24 AM Eastern. RETEST-1 reports 14 of 18 items passed; the other four include a child delete-screen failure, sidecar subtitles selected but not drawn, inconsistent online Next and network-dependent offline wording. Folder poster/NFO handling has no recorded repair. No new feature tick is justified. Nothing on this page says they are fixed.

What this changed in the numbers. No check was added. One was withdrawn: the Real-world check on “Library, player and web player” (80% to 60%), because its newest test failed. Four rows were added (Photos and phone backups, Family achievements, Picture frame, Kitchen announcements), each at 40% (Built and Automated tests only), so the average across features moved from 54% to 53% (66 to 70 features). Code built and passing automated tests: 91% to 92%. Tested on real devices: 28% to 26%. Confirmed working on real devices: 0 to 0. Group averages: Android phone app: 60% to 59%; Windows desktop app: 54% to 51%; Other systems: 47% to 47%; Services and website: 41% to 41%. Labels: Watch Together moved from In development to Beta (it has worked on two real phones in four runs); Posters and info, Beebo Auto and Library, player and web player moved from Available to Beta.

Reported by the owner, not a helper run: on Oct 22, 2026 Nick watched House season 4 episode 2 on a 70-inch Samsung smart TV through a Google Chromecast. Numbers come from our own test list (last updated Oct 24, 2026, 9:15 PM Eastern) and were worked out on Oct 24, 2026, 9:15 PM Eastern. They show how much of our checklist has passed, not whether the app is bug-free. A high code number means the code is built and its automated tests pass. It does not mean nothing is left to build: the open code items are listed under each bar.

Code built and tests passing is the share of the Built and Automated tests checks that passed. The separate Device and real-world checks recorded figure is the share of Real device, Real-world check and Ready to call Available that passed. It includes AI tests on real devices; it does not mean a person checked every feature. The final Ready check needs a human look, recorded by Nick. These separate figures keep their existing checklist method. Group figures are the average of their features and are never rounded up to 100. A feature shows "Code 100%" only when both of those code checks passed and no CODE item is still open; it counts as "Confirmed working on real devices" only when all five checks passed. Remaining counts are open road-to-100 items. All 70 features are counted, including those that are planned or on hold.

How to read this page

Four kinds of evidence. No shortcuts.

We separate what a check actually established from what is still unknown. A completed step is not automatically a passed feature, and a local result is not the same as a real-device result.

✓

Real-device confirmed

A named build was tried on a stated device, connection and feature path. It confirms that specific situation—not every setup.

⌁

Built or locally checked

Code-level and local checks help catch regressions. A person still needs to verify the experience in the packaged app.

?

Verification pending

There is not enough reviewed evidence to call this a pass or a failure yet. It stays visible instead of becoming an optimistic claim.

×

Needs more people or hardware

Some paths require another person, another device, a receiver, a tuner or payment access. One session cannot validate them alone.

Progress · last updated Oct 12, 2026, 12:05 PM Eastern

How much has been checked, and what is left.

How to read the percentages: each one is checks passed out of checks planned for that area. It measures how much of our test list has passed. It is not a measure of how finished or bug-free the product is, and 100% means every listed check passed, not that nothing can go wrong. The feature scores in the tables further down are weighted: Built 20, Automated tests 20, Real device 25, Real-world check 25 and Ready to call Available 10, which only Nick can set. 90% means the code is built, it passed the phone or PC test and no error is known; the only thing left is Nick saying Ready. Every row says when it was last tested and what is still left. Progress by feature · last updated Oct 24, 2026, 9:15 PM Eastern

Every feature: how far along, and what gets it to 100%.

How the percentage works. Each feature has five checks. Built is worth 24 points. Passing automated tests adds 25. Running on a real phone or PC adds 25. The real-world check adds 25. Our test process uses AI testers on real devices for that check. At 99%, all four checks have passed and no known error is open. That means tested and confirmed working within the recorded checks. The feature is considered fully coded and working. The last 1% is a human look, not more building. Nick or a tester looks with their own eyes and says Ready. Only Nick records the Ready check. An open known error caps the score at 90%. Existing record limits can keep it lower. At 99%, the label is "Tested and working, waiting for a human look". At 100%, it is "Ready, a human confirmed". Each row says what was proven, when it was tested and what remains unverified.

A tick means the check passed on the date shown. A dash means it has not. The last column lists what is still unverified. Automated tests alone prove only the Built and Automated tests checks. The public status remains Available, Beta, In development or Planned.

Updated 13 of 70 features changed since the previous update (Oct 24, 2026, 4:32 AM Eastern). Highlighted rows (only those changed in this latest update) show the old and new percentage (the small mark on the bar is the old position), what changed in one sentence, and the date and time in Eastern. Older changes stay in the last column as “Earlier update” notes. This temporary marker is shown while testing is under way.

How to read the “Left to reach 100%” column

100% means all five checks passed, including a human look recorded by Nick. 99% means tested and working within the recorded checks, waiting for that human look. Each row has a one-line summary and an expandable box, “What 100% means and what is left”, with what is already done and what is still to do. The remaining work comes in four kinds:

  • 🛠️ Build or fix (we can do this): code or tests that a developer or AI helper can write.
  • 📱 Test on a real device: needs a real phone, PC, TV or car; a computer test cannot replace it.
  • 🧑 Needs Nick: his decision, an account, money or a legal question.
  • 🚧 Blocked: waiting on something else, such as a careful security review or a decision.

Remaining items across all 70 features: 39 to build or fix, 89 real-device tests, 22 for Nick, 26 blocked.

What does “mostly proven” mean?

Every feature is checked five ways: Built (it exists in the code, 24 points), Automated tests (computer tests pass, 25), Real device (it ran on a real phone, PC or TV, 25), Real-world check (AI testers used real devices in a realistic situation, 25) and Ready to call Available (Nick records that he or a tester has looked and said Ready, 1).

  • Green, 80 to 100%, “mostly proven”: almost everything has been shown to work; the open check is listed in the last column. At 99% the remaining point is a human look, recorded by Nick. The dated limits in each row still apply.
  • Gold shine, 100%, “ready, a human confirmed”: every check passed and a person recorded Ready. Rows only turn gold after that human confirmation.
  • Purple glow, 99%, “tested and working, waiting for a human look”: all four automatic checks passed. The last point is a person looking and saying Ready, which only Nick records. The dated limits in each row still apply.
  • Yellow, 40 to 79%, “partly proven”: built and tested by computer. The real-device or real-world proof is still to come.
  • Red, 0 to 39%, “early or not started”: little or nothing has passed yet.

A bar is not a promise that a feature is finished or free of bugs. It only counts how many of our own checks have passed, and a check only counts when we have real evidence for it.

Colour key Green 80 to 100%: mostly proven Yellow 40 to 79%: partly proven Red 0 to 39%: early or not started Purple glow, 99%: tested and working, waiting for a human look Gold shine, 100%: ready, a human confirmed Gold shine = 100%. A human (Nick) has looked and recorded Ready; nothing is at 100% until that happens. Purple glow = 99%. Every check we can run ourselves has passed (built, computer tests, a real device and a real-world check); only the last point is left, a human look recorded by Nick. At 99% the tag reads “Tested and working, waiting for a human look”; at 100% it reads “Ready, a human confirmed”. Colour never replaces the words: every row also says its percentage and its tier. Small boxes show the five checks: a tick means passed, a dash means not yet.

When we tested, what we found.

We keep earlier results so you can see testing is ongoing, including the problems it found. Times are Eastern.

Oct 24, 2026, 9:15 PM EasternReal-world checks on Oct 24: a second household through Beebo Relay, Chromecast on the TV, browser captions, and honest failures

What was tested: Real devices, Oct 24, 2026 (afternoon and evening, Eastern). A Galaxy S24 on 5G mobile data to the owner’s home server (direct about 1:30 PM, Beebo Relay about 3:40 PM) and to a second household’s old 4-core Windows PC running Windows 0.1.102 (about 7:50 to 8:05 PM). The S24 and the Living Room TV on unpublished Android test builds (controls fix about 1:00 to 2:40 PM; a streamed film on test build 42 about 8:20 PM). The browser player on the S24 over 5G through Beebo Relay (about 8:25 PM), Chrome on a PC, a Galaxy S9+ and the S24 for Campsite, and two phones for Watch Together.

What happened: Passed within the scope stated: direct and Beebo Relay playback from a phone on 5G; a real second household’s PC through Beebo Relay playing the original file (about 2 seconds to connect); the cast controls working by finger on the real TV and a streamed film cast with picture and sound on test build 42; three subtitle cues drawing in the browser player; a no-app Campsite guest joining from a phone browser; Chrome playback, pause, arrow keys and quality changes. FAILED or not working: a converted 720p quality failed twice on the second household’s old PC; casting a downloaded copy did not play on the TV; Watch Together did not connect for phones signed in only to the home server (test build 41 now says so honestly); Trip Journal “Save as a video” stayed at 0% on a Galaxy S9+; and the web player had keyboard-focus, show-page, blank Suggestions and owner-only-control failures (fixes written, not merged). Not tested: Wi-Fi to mobile-data hand-over, sessions over a few minutes, the phone pass-through cast route, seeking by dragging and captions on the TV, the Android app’s own captions, a guest game round or guest video in Campsite, the Android photo map on a phone, a second person on Relay. Oct 13, 2026 (Eastern)Full device pass: two Samsung phones, a Windows PC and a real Chromecast; three failures found, several fixes written but not yet published

What was tested: A full device pass on Oct 13, 2026, about 7:00 AM to 1:15 PM Eastern, by a helper driving the devices with the owner nearby: a Samsung Galaxy S24 Ultra, a Samsung Galaxy S9+ (Android 10), the Windows dev PC, a home computer running the published desktop 0.1.87, and a real Chromecast on the Living Room TV, on home Wi-Fi and on 5G mobile data. Builds: the published downloads are Windows 0.1.87 and Android 1.51-tester22 (build 72). The phones ran a test build of the current code made that morning (tester22 plus some unmerged fixes; the app still shows 1.51-tester22) and the PC ran the unpublished 0.1.88 test build, so none of this counts as a test of a published download. An earlier same-morning two-phone Campsite and phone-speakers test, and a Sea Battle game played by two people, are included in the rows.

What happened: Passed on real devices: all five phone tabs on both phones; films (resume, play, pause, skip, quality menu, floating player on the S9+, 2x speed); a film on 5G through the direct route and through Beebo Relay (the relay first had to be switched on for the test account on the server, which the owner did); Watch Together on two phones (host, viewer, sync after a 45-second skip, leave and rejoin); a Car Party host and a passenger page on a second phone; Campsite hosting, a blank-name guest named "Guest 1", a guest reconnecting after 15 seconds offline, and 42 of 42 games opening (41 starting with a computer opponent; Crazy Eights played by hand); Chromecast with a person watching the TV (picture, sound, pause, play, skip both ways, a quality change and stop); radio and a podcast playing; a computer-made audiobook with chapters, jumps and speeds; sample music on both phones; playlists, watchlist, collections, Library health, Family Dashboard, Camp Jobs, a Packing List synced between two phones, Family Recipes; accessibility switches including Caregiver view; six languages on a phone and Spanish on the PC; the offline demo; the Share the app QR scan, download and install on the S9+; a trip link that opened on 5G with a photo and a clip once the away-from-home setting was on; all 27 Windows pages, five Home layouts, Customize layout, the update check and the signed-in browser player (17 of 17 pages, 720p/480p switching); phone speakers on two phones as a stereo pair. Failed: following a new podcast ("Your Beebo computer had a problem", three tries); the Trip Journal "Save as a video" (failed twice on the test build; with a fix on a branch it worked on the S24 for a 1-card and a 13-second trip, but the videos were not played back); the desktop Music window stayed silent at 0:00 (cause found and a fix written, not merged or re-tested in the real window); the ordinary trip link served from the home computer did not open from outside the home on 5G because the router did not let it in. Found, still open: the sound stops with the screen off by default (the Screen off: On option keeps it playing); the Music screen's Albums/Artists/Songs buttons overlap the "Mixes and radio" heading on both phones; a shared downloaded video was silent on a guest phone; the demo player has no subtitles button although its page says it does; phone volume buttons do not control the TV volume while casting; the live Car Party page still refuses a blank nickname (the automatic name is fixed in the code but not on the server); phone-speaker pages show a false Bluetooth warning and the PC speaker seat did not play in the surround test; no screen lets a customer switch Beebo Relay on for their own account. Not tested: Beebo Auto in a car, Android TV, Fire TV, TalkBack, a real wake of a sleeping PC, Live TV with a tuner, Chromecast to other TVs, converted films while casting, a first install from the QR on a phone that never had Beebo, accepting a library share, Walkie-talkie from anywhere, a real TV pairing code, Remote Desktop, Jellyfin, parental rules, an update installed over an older version, subtitles, and the new fixes on real devices. Ready to call Available was not ticked for any row, because the exact published downloads were not the ones tested.

What was tested: Nick hosted Campsite while a friend on a Samsung S20 created Sea Battle. Nick joined over DIRECT-BeeboTV Wi-Fi (BeeboDirect), the no-internet Wi-Fi Beebo creates for Campsite.

Scope and not tested: This is human testing for Sea Battle only; the other 40 Campsite games have scripted-guest coverage only. Nick's phone, both browsers, game length, and sound or graphics observations were not recorded. A phone hotspot and a guest on mobile data remain untested.

Oct 12, 2026 (Eastern)Two-home real-device test: phone on 5G, home Wi-Fi and a friend’s Wi-Fi; relay, two viewers, library sharing, Windows hand test

What was tested: A two-home test on Oct 12, 2026: a Windows PC at each of two separate homes on two different networks, one family server at a third home, a Samsung Galaxy S24 Ultra on home Wi-Fi, on 5G mobile data and at a friend’s Wi-Fi, a Galaxy S9+, and a browser on the S24 and on a PC. Builds: the published Android 1.51-tester22 (build 72), Windows desktop 0.1.87 (published) and a 0.1.88 test build that is not published.

What passed: Away-from-home playback (a direct connection to a home PC worked; a connection through Beebo Relay worked from a phone on 5G once switched on, with the first picture in about 3 seconds; the phone browser on 5G also played); play, pause, resume (a 63-second pause held its exact position), seek and quality switching to 480p in the phone browser and in the phone app; two viewers watching at once from one home PC, both connected directly in about a second; the phone app kept playing across Wi-Fi to 5G to Wi-Fi switches and through 24 seconds of airplane mode; sharing a library with another home (send an invite, accept, play, and “stop sharing” refusing new playback within about 30 seconds); the online games lobby loaded; uploading from the phone to the home PC (Space Saver backup: 3 files arrived); browse sort and filters, favourites and watchlist, playlists, radio, podcasts, Campsite game-room coverage using scripted guests (not a hand-played result for another game), dark theme, landscape and restart. On this website, 67 of 67 home-page links returned OK, and the installer and APK sizes matched the update feeds.

Windows hand test: On the 0.1.88 test build (not published), about 35 screens were opened and looked right (Home, Movies, TV Shows, Trailers, Discover, Live TV, Audiobooks, Podcasts, Radio, Music, Photos, Upload, Switch to Beebo, the admin pages, Users, Watch History, Story Studio and all eight Settings pages). Not yet done: Log out on screen, the signed-in browser player, subtitles and keyboard-only use.

What we found (fixed in the code): A shared-library guest could be dropped to a sign-in screen with no way back to their own home; a stopped share showed a wrong message and still looked active in the guest’s list; the web player froze for minutes after a phone switched from Wi-Fi to mobile data; the home PC could stop accepting viewers after many sessions because ports were not released; the app’s own picture-in-picture window did not close properly; some text was clipped at 150% text size; Library health showed a misleading counter and a stale “cannot be reached” line; skip-intro jumped over a 2:39 opening scene on one show; Auto quality chose 480p on a fast home connection; a share with downloads switched off still offered a download button.

What we found (still open): A 40 to 70 second freeze after seeking far into a long converted film (a safeguard is written, but the cause is not fully confirmed); voices slightly out of sync after jumps on one TV episode (a likely cause was found and fixed in the code, but it is not confirmed on the real episode); the Read It To Me buttons were hidden behind the bottom bar (a fix is in progress); a new home name could not get its secure certificate until it was registered by hand; casting from a browser to a friend’s Chromecast was blocked because that TV is not yet registered for Beebo’s test receiver (casting to a TV through the phone app was proven earlier on the home TV).

Not tested: Chromecast to the friend’s TV, Beebo Auto in a car, Watch Together with a second device, a real TV pairing code (Link a TV is switched off on the server; a wrong code was refused with a plain message), TalkBack, two people on Beebo Relay at once, the Windows Log out button on screen, a House-to-House share started from a PC, and the new fixes on real devices. The exact counted paths are in the progress table.

Oct 23, 2026 (Eastern)New scoring: a 90% mark for features that are built and fully tested, waiting for Nick

What was tested: No new test was run. This is a change to how the percentage is worked out, set by Nick on Oct 23, 2026.

What happened: The five checks now have different weights: Built 20, Automated tests 20, Real device 25, Real-world check 25 and Ready to call Available 10. The first four add up to 90. A feature reaches 90 only when all four passed and it has no open known problem; with an open known problem it stays at 85 or below. Only Nick can add the last 10 by saying a feature is Ready, so no feature is at 100. No check was added or withdrawn. 7 features are now at 90% ("Built and fully tested, waiting for Nick"). Oct 9, 2026 (Eastern)Android 1.51-tester17 hands-on test on one Galaxy S9+: 17 of 20 checks passed, 1 did not, 2 not run

What was tested: Android 1.51-tester17 (build 67), installed over tester16 on a Samsung Galaxy S9+ (Android 10) by cable; home Wi-Fi only.

What happened: Passed: the install over the older build kept the signed-in account; cold start to Home in under a second; all five tabs and search; the Connection route screen (home Wi-Fi, direct); play, pause, resume and seek forward and back; the floating picture-in-picture window when pressing Home during a film; the quality picker and the subtitle menu opening (the film tried had no built-in subtitles); audio kept playing with the screen off when the “Screen off” switch was turned on; progress kept after force-closing the app; Audio only mode with Home; no crash was logged. Did not pass: on this phone “Screen off” was Off when first seen, so audio stopped when the screen turned off until the switch was turned on; we could not tell whether that was an earlier choice or the default on a fresh install, so the new-install default is untested. Not run: rotating back to portrait, and the watchlist star (not reachable in the landscape dialog). Note: Surprise me starts a random film straight away rather than showing a title page. Not tested: the Galaxy S24 Ultra, a car, a TV, mobile data or Beebo Relay, and a fresh install.

Oct 3, 2026 (morning, Eastern)Hand test by the owner on a Galaxy S24 Ultra and a Galaxy S9+ (builds of the night before)

What was tested: Hand test by the owner on a Galaxy S24 Ultra and a Galaxy S9+ (builds of the night before)

What happened: Passed: signing out and back in, with Continue watching still the owner's own; a second household member sees only their own Continue watching; seeking on the phone and while casting to a TV, with no lag; picture-in-picture after pressing Home; sound kept playing with the screen off during a video; Library health says the home server is older. Largest text size looked fine except for the bottom navigation bar (a fix is written, not yet seen on a screen). Car party: the passenger join code said the password was wrong on a guest phone (most likely an older saved network; a hint was added), and the app showed no videos or posters after joining the camp Wi-Fi, which has no internet (fixed in code, not yet re-tested). A 720p download looked like the original because the running row did not show its quality (the row now says 720p). Not reported yet: mobile-data route, Watch Together rejoin after closing the app, audiobooks (none in this library), whether a cast starts paused.

Oct 3, 2026 (morning, Eastern)Linux full test run (Ubuntu 24.04 under Windows), worker, service and desktop suites

What was tested: Linux full test run (Ubuntu 24.04 under Windows), worker, service and desktop suites

What happened: No Linux-only bugs: no path, case, permission or signal failures. Worker 685 of 685 and its probe test passed; the worker suite on the Node service port 683 of 683. The service's own suite had 4 failures, a real gap from a new setting that was not registered in the service config, found by a new drift test and fixed the same morning (it now shows 316 passing and 1 failing, which has not been examined). Desktop: 537 test files passed; one needs a real Electron install, which the Linux copy did not have; one known timing test passed on its automatic retry. Not tried: a real Linux desktop session, ARM, Raspberry Pi.

What was tested: Automated tests on the newest code (Windows)

What happened: Desktop suite in three parts: 226, 158 and 158 test files passed, 0 failed, 1 known skip. Worker suite 700 of 700; the same suite on the service port 697 of 697. New this morning: a test that every data-changing page and route refuses a visitor, a restricted profile and a plain member where it should (621 paths, 2,593 probes, none open); owner settings survive a restart and bad values are refused (9 tests); request-size limits on every worker route; payment webhooks reject replays, tampering and bad bodies; logs hold no tokens, emails or addresses; sign-out clears the previous person's watch positions on the phone; downloads and photo backup are tied to the signed-in account. Several security fixes are merged but NOT yet on the live server (they need a deploy).

Oct 3, 2026 (morning, Eastern)Volume leveler: synthetic benchmark

What was tested: Volume leveler: synthetic benchmark

What happened: On a generated 16-second clip with a very loud burst, both Basic and Strong flatten a 26 dB loud-to-quiet swing to about 1.5 dB and each processes about 40 times faster than real time on one thread. This does not separate Basic from Strong and is not a listening test; a real episode on real speakers is still needed.

Oct 3, 2026 (early morning Eastern)Walkie-talkie internet connection tests on Linux

What was tested: Walkie-talkie internet connection tests on Linux

What happened: Two failing checks traced to a real bug and fixed. When the host removed a guest it closed the connection and its data channels at the same moment, which threw away the pending close signals, so the guest never saw the channel close on Linux (Windows passed by timing). The host now closes the channels first, then the connection. All 28 related checks pass on Linux and on Windows.

What this record establishes

Specific paths on specific phones

The S24 Ultra exercised development-build playback. The S9+ exercised the published test build offline and found problems. Neither session verifies every feature, Android device or connection route. The device sessions did not record counted checklist totals; automatic test counts are reported separately above.

Known limits of a solo pass

Not something one session can prove

  • Campsite guest joins and shared play need another participating device.
  • Casting needs a compatible receiver and a real network route.
  • Live TV needs the relevant tuner and broadcast setup.
  • Paid-service flows need authorised payment test access.
Browser test pages · October 1, 2026

Microphone checks on both phones; camera on S24 only.

These Phase 0 test pages checked browser prerequisites for the planned walkie-talkie. They used a test certificate, not the Android host's own certificate, and exchanged hold-to-talk audio with a headless Chrome browser on the PC. They do not verify a released walkie-talkie feature.

Human testing remains essential

Turn one result into useful evidence.

01

Use the invited build

Record the app version, distribution and the device you used before you begin.

02

Try one clear path

Note the feature, connection route and what you expected to happen.

03

Report what happened

Successes and failures both help when the steps can be repeated.

04

Keep private data private

Remove passwords, access codes, private media names and personal details before sharing.
